=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given two integer arrays, skill and mana, of length n and m, respectively.
In a laboratory, n wizards must brew m potions in order. Each potion has a mana capacity mana[j] and must pass through all the wizards sequentially to be brewed properly. The time taken by the i^th wizard on the j^th potion is time_ij = skill[i] * mana[j].
Since the brewing process is delicate, a potion must be passed to the next wizard immediately after the current wizard completes their work. This means the timing must be synchronized so that each wizard begins working on a potion exactly when it arrives. ​
Return the minimum amount of time required for the potions to be brewed properly.
 
Example 1:

Input: skill = [1,5,2,4], mana = [5,1,4,2]
Output: 110
Explanation:



Potion Number
Start time
Wizard 0 done by
Wizard 1 done by
Wizard 2 done by
Wizard 3 done by


0
0
5
30
40
60


1
52
53
58
60
64


2
54
58
78
86
102


3
86
88
98
102
110



As an example for why wizard 0 cannot start working on the 1^st potion before time t = 52, consider the case where the wizards started preparing the 1^st potion at time t = 50. At time t = 58, wizard 2 is done with the 1^st potion, but wizard 3 will still be working on the 0^th potion till time t = 60.

Example 2:

Input: skill = [1,1,1], mana = [1,1,1]
Output: 5
Explanation:

Preparation of the 0^th potion begins at time t = 0, and is completed by time t = 3.
Preparation of the 1^st potion begins at time t = 1, and is completed by time t = 4.
Preparation of the 2^nd potion begins at time t = 2, and is completed by time t = 5.


Example 3:

Input: skill = [1,2,3,4], mana = [1,2]
Output: 21

 
Constraints:

n == skill.length
m == mana.length
1 <= n, m <= 5000
1 <= mana[i], skill[i] <= 5000

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.


Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Pseudo:
class Solution:
    def minTime(self, skill: List[int], mana: List[int]) -> int:
        n=len(skill); m=len(mana)
        pref=[0]*(n+1)
        for i,s in enumerate(skill):
            pref[i+1]=pref[i]+s
        total=pref[n]
        cur=0
        for j in range(m-1):
            a=mana[j]; b=mana[j+1]
            # compute max_i a*pref[i+1] - b*pref[i]
            # i 0..n-1
            best=0? Need i=0: a*pref[1] - b*pref[0] = a*skill[0] >0, so best positive. But if n? n>=1. Could initialize best = a*pref[1].
            # loop i from 1 to n-1? Let's use for idx in range(1,n): val=a*pref[idx+1]-b*pref[idx]
            # But pref[0]=0, pref[1]=skill0.
            best = a * pref[1]  # i=0
            for idx in range(1, n):
                val = a * pref[idx+1] - b * pref[idx]
                if val > best: best = val
            cur += best
        return cur + mana[-1]*total

Since potions flow in order, for each wizard i, start time of potion j at wizard i is s_j + mana_j*P_i. It must be >= completion time of potion j-1 at wizard i:

s_j - s_{j-1} >= mana_{j-1}*P_{i+1} - mana_j*P_i for all i.
Minimal s_j is longest path. Since graph is chain, recurrence. Good.

Could use Li Chao / convex hull to compute F(a,b) for all queries (a,b) in O((n+q) log C). Let's explore.

Could be faster. But implementing convex hull in final code more complex, risk bugs. But maybe not needed. Let's maybe derive formal proof for ourselves:
Let `P_i` prefix. For potion j, let `x_j` be start time at wizard0. Then wizard i begins potion j at `x_j + mana_j P_i` and finishes at `x_j + mana_j P_{i+1}`. Since each wizard processes potions in order and cannot overlap, for each i and j:
`x_{j+1} + mana_{j+1} P_i >= x_j + mana_j P_{i+1}`.
Rearrange:
`x_{j+1} >= x_j + mana_j P_{i+1} - mana_{j+1} P_i`.
The minimal feasible `x_{j+1}` is the maximum RHS. Starting with `x_0=0` and applying greedily gives minimal because each step lower bound is necessary and sufficient. Answer `x_{m-1}+mana_{m-1}P_n`.

This is enough.

Now, let's think about code style. We can write:

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minTime(self, skill: List[int], mana: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(skill)
        m = len(mana)

        pref = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i, s in enumerate(skill):
            pref[i + 1] = pref[i] + s

        cur = 0
        p = pref
        for j in range(m - 1):
            a = mana[j]
            b = mana[j + 1]
            best = a * p[1]
            # i = 1 .. n-1
            for i in range(1, n):
                val = a * p[i + 1] - b * p[i]
                if val > best:
                    best = val
            cur += best

        return cur + mana[-1] * p[n]

Potential micro-optimization: Use `p_i = p[i]`? In loop:
for i in range(1, n):
    val = a * p[i+1] - b * p[i]
This accesses p[i+1], p[i]. Could use `for i in range(n-1): val = a * p[i+1] - b * p[i]` if we initialize best for i=n-1? Let's see. If loop i from 0 to n-1:
for i in range(n):
    val = a * p[i+1] - b * p[i]
This is n iterations, no special best. But branch. Could initialize best = -10**30 and loop all n. This avoids p[1] special and range(1,n) but does n iterations instead of n-1, negligible. However branch inside. Maybe:
best = -10**30
for i in range(n):
    val = a * p[i+1] - b * p[i]
    if val > best:
        best = val
This is simpler and less risk. But `best = -10**30` maybe not enough? Values up to 1e11, so -1e30 safe. But if constraints changed, Python int. Could use `best = None` or `-10**100`. Better initialize with first term to avoid sentinel. The first term is always valid. I prefer `best = a * p[1]` and loop from 1.

Could also use `for i in range(1, n):` if n=1 no loop. Good.

Let's maybe think about "potion must be passed to the next wizard immediately after the current wizard completes their work" meaning there is no waiting time between wizards for a potion. Now, maybe there is a known "minimum time" can be computed by considering each wizard as a machine with jobs in order and no wait between machines. This is a flow shop with no wait? Actually "no wait" flow shop: jobs cannot wait between machines. We are scheduling start times to minimize makespan with permutation flow shop and no wait. For two machines, Johnson's rule; for m machines, etc. But here processing times are product of machine-dependent skill and job-dependent mana, a special "proportional" flow shop. Our recurrence is the standard no-wait flow shop makespan for a fixed job order (given order). The minimal start time for job j is max over machines of completion time of previous job minus processing time of current job before that machine. Yes. This is exactly no-wait flow shop with fixed sequence. The recurrence is correct.

Wait, in no-wait flow shop, for a fixed job order, the start time of job j is determined by the maximum of `C_{i,j-1} - sum_{h< i} p_{h,j}`. Our formula uses `C_{i,j-1} = s_{j-1} + sum_{h<=i} p_{h,j-1}` and arrival at machine i = s_j + sum_{h<i} p_{h,j}`. So `s_j >= s_{j-1} + sum_{h<=i} p_{h,j-1} - sum_{h<i} p_{h,j}`. Our `mana[j-1]*P_{i+1} - mana[j]*P_i` exactly. Good.

Now, let's think if there is any need to consider the first potion's start time relative to previous jobs? No.

Let's ensure code uses `List` imported. If the judge already imports, duplicate okay. Code:

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minTime(self, skill: List[int], mana: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(skill)
        m = len(mana)

        pref = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i, s in enumerate(skill):
            pref[i + 1] = pref[i] + s

        cur = 0
        p = pref

        for j in range(m - 1):
            a = mana[j]
            b = mana[j + 1]
            best = a * p[1]
            for i in range(1, n):
                v = a * p[i + 1] - b * p[i]
                if v > best:
                    best = v
            cur += best

        return cur + mana[-1] * p[n]

j=1 a=1,b=1 best=1; cur=2. ans=2+1*3=5. Good.

Actually around a convex polygon, dot product as a function of vertex index is cyclic unimodal: it has one maximum and one minimum, so if you traverse from min to max it increases, then decreases to min. If you start at min, sequence increases then decreases (unimodal). We don't know min. Could use binary search for maximum in a circular unimodal array. But implementing robustly with collinear edges maybe tricky.

Alternatively, because points are sorted by x, we can compute the upper hull as a concave piecewise-linear function and query maximum of a*X + b*Y. Let's derive a simpler query using slopes.

For a convex polygon, maximum dot product for direction d occurs at a vertex where the supporting line with normal d touches. The edge slopes around hull are monotonic. We can binary search on edge slopes. Let's define hull vertices in CCW order. For each edge from v_k to v_{k+1}, the direction of the edge has angle. The dot product along the edge is linear; the maximum over the edge occurs at one endpoint depending on sign of dot(d, edge_vector). If dot(d, edge_vector) > 0, moving along edge increases dot, so max is at next vertex; if <0, at current. At the maximum vertex, the two adjacent edges have dot(d, edge_in) >=0 and dot(d, edge_out) <=0 (depending orientation). We can binary search for the vertex where edge slopes cross.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is a more efficient way to compute `best` using `max` over `i` but with `p` as a list and `a,b` local. We can write:
for j in range(m - 1):
    a = mana[j]
    b = mana[j + 1]
    best = a * p[1]
    for i in range(1, n):
        val = a * p[i + 1] - b * p[i]
        if val > best:
            best = val
    cur += best
This is clear.

Could we use `for i in range(n - 1, 0, -1)`? no.

Now, let's maybe explore the convex hull approach just in case O(n*m) TLE. If we can implement a simpler query using precomputed upper hull and binary search, maybe final code more robust time-wise. But risk of bug. Let's see if we can design a simple and correct hull query.

We have points `(X_i, Y_i) = (P_{i+1}, -P_i)`, i=0..n-1. Need max `a*X + b*Y` for each query (a,b). Since a,b positive. We can build the upper convex hull of points with respect to maximizing `a*X + b*Y`. Actually for any direction d=(a,b), the maximum over points is on the convex hull. We can build full convex hull and then use ternary search? Let's see if we can use the fact that the points are sorted by X and the direction has positive components. The maximum dot product will be on the "upper-right" part of the hull? Since a,b>0, direction points up-right. But Y is negative, so "up" means less negative (smaller i), "right" means larger i. The maximum could be at any hull vertex on the upper hull from leftmost to rightmost? In example, for a=1,b=4, max at B(6,-1), which is on upper hull between A and D? Full hull A,C,D,B. B is on upper hull (edge D-B-A). C is lower hull. So maximum for positive direction lies on the upper hull (the chain from leftmost to rightmost that is above). The upper hull of points sorted by x is the set of points that form the upper envelope. For example1, upper hull is A,B,D? Wait earlier full hull A,C,D,B; upper chain from leftmost A to rightmost D is A-B-D? Let's compute upper hull using standard upper: reversed D,B,A? Actually upper hull from left to right should be A,B,D? Let's check points: A(1,0), B(6,-1), D(12,-8). Are they collinear? Slopes A-B=-0.2, B-D=(-8+1)/(12-6)=-7/6=-1.167. The chain A-B-D is above the lower chain A-C-D. Yes upper hull is A,B,D. C is below. So upper hull size 3. For a=1,b=4, max at B on upper hull. For a=5,b=1, max at D on upper hull. For a maybe very large b? max at A. So we only need upper hull (max y for x) because b>0 favors larger Y (less negative) and a>0 favors larger X; the maximum dot product over all points for positive direction should be on the upper convex hull (the set of points that are not below any segment between others). Is that always true? Suppose a point is below the upper hull; can it maximize a*X+b*Y for positive a,b? If it's below the segment between two upper hull points, then for any positive a,b, the dot product at the point is less than or equal to the linear interpolation of dot products at endpoints? Let's check. If point C lies below segment AB (where A.x < C.x < B.x), then C_y < linear interpolation. For a,b>0, dot(C) = a*C_x + b*C_y < a*C_x + b*(linear y) = linear interpolation of dot(A), dot(B) (since dot is linear in x,y along segment). Thus dot(C) < max(dot(A), dot(B)). So C cannot be maximum. Therefore only upper hull points matter. Great.

So we can build the upper convex hull of points sorted by x, keeping points that make counter-clockwise? Let's define upper hull as the chain from leftmost to rightmost with slopes decreasing? For example A(1,0), B(6,-1), D(12,-8): slopes A-B=-0.2, B-D=-1.167, slopes decreasing (more negative). The upper hull is concave (slopes decreasing). To build, iterate points in increasing x, maintain hull such that the slope between last two is greater than slope between last and new? Let's derive.

We want upper hull (max y). Standard monotonic chain upper: for p in sorted:
while len >=2 and cross(hull[-2], hull[-1], p) >= 0: pop

Let's test with A,B,D. A,B; add D: cross(A,B,D) = (B-A)x(D-B) = (5,-1)x(6,-7) = 5*(-7) - (-1*6) = -35+6=-29 <0. Standard upper with cross >=0 pop? Since -29 <0, keep. Upper hull A,B,D. Good. If we had C(8,-6) before D: A,B,C cross(A,B,C)=(5,-1)x(2,-5)=-23 <0 keep? Upper hull A,B,C? But C is below segment B-D? Let's see with only A,B,C, is C on upper hull? Points A(1,0),B(6,-1),C(8,-6). Slope B-C=-2.5, slope A-B=-0.2, slopes decreasing, so C is on upper hull? But is C below segment A-B? No, x=8 beyond B, so it's rightmost. It is on upper hull for these three. When D added, C may be popped because cross(B,C,D)? Let's compute upper with standard:
Start A,B,C. Add D: cross(B,C,D) = (C-B)x(D-C) = (2,-5)x(4,-2)=2*(-2)-(-5*4)=-4+20=16 >=0, pop C. Then cross(A,B,D) = -29 <0 keep. Upper A,B,D. So standard upper with pop while cross >=0 works. Good.

So build upper hull:
upper = []
for p in points: # sorted by x increasing
    while len(upper) >= 2 and cross(upper[-2], upper[-1], p) >= 0:
        upper.pop()
    upper.append(p)
This yields upper hull from leftmost to rightmost with clockwise? For example A,B,D. Good.

Now, query max `a*x + b*y` over upper hull. The upper hull is a concave chain with slopes decreasing. The dot product along the hull as a function of vertex index is unimodal (increases then decreases) because the slopes of edges are decreasing. More precisely, for an edge from v_k to v_{k+1}, the change in dot is `a*(x_{k+1}-x_k) + b*(y_{k+1}-y_k)`. Since x increases, y decreases. The sign of this change depends on the edge slope `s_k = (y_{k+1}-y_k)/(x_{k+1}-x_k)`. Change = (x_{k+1}-x_k) * (a + b*s_k). Since x diff >0, sign is sign of `a + b*s_k`. The slopes s_k are decreasing along upper hull. Therefore the sequence of changes starts positive (if a + b*s_0 >0) and eventually becomes negative (since s_k -> -inf maybe). Thus dot values increase while `a + b*s_k > 0`, then decrease. We can find the maximum vertex by binary searching for the last edge with `a + b*s_k >= 0` (or first with <=0). The maximum is at vertex k+1? Let's define edges from v_k to v_{k+1}, k=0..h-2. If change >0, dot(v_{k+1}) > dot(v_k), so max to the right. If change <0, max to the left. The maximum vertex is the one where incoming edge change >=0 and outgoing edge change <=0. So we can binary search on edge slopes.

Since h <= n <=5000, we could even do O(h) per query? That's O(n*m) again. But we can do binary search because slopes decreasing. Need handle integer arithmetic to avoid floating.

Let `upper` list of points (x,y). Slopes between consecutive points are decreasing. For query (a,b), define `f(k) = a + b * (y_{k+1}-y_k)/(x_{k+1}-x_k)`. We want find largest k such that f(k) >= 0? Let's test.

Example upper A(1,0), B(6,-1), D(12,-8). Slopes: s0=-1/5=-0.2, s1=-7/6=-1.1667.
Query a=1,b=4: f0 = 1 + 4*(-0.2)=0.2 >0, f1=1+4*(-1.1667)= -3.667 <0. So dot increases from A to B, decreases from B to D. Max at B (vertex 1). Largest k with f(k)>=0 is k=0, max vertex = k+1 =1. Good.
Query a=5,b=1: f0=5-0.2=4.8>0, f1=5-1.1667=3.833>0. Both positive, dot increases all the way, max at last vertex D (index 2). Largest k with f>=0 is k=1, max vertex = k+1=2. Good.
Query a=1,b=100? f0=1-20=-19<0, dot decreases from start, max at first vertex A. Largest k with f>=0? none, maybe -1, max vertex=0. Good.

So algorithm:
- Build upper hull points.
- For each query (a,b):
  - Binary search over edge indices 0..h-2 to find the last edge where `a * dx + b * dy >= 0` (since dx>0, f>=0 equivalent to a*dx + b*dy >=0). Note dy can be negative.
  - If no such edge, max at vertex 0.
  - Else let k = last edge with nonnegative change. The maximum vertex is k+1? Wait if last edge with nonnegative change is k, then dot increases from v_k to v_{k+1}, and for edge k+1 (if exists) change negative, so max at v_{k+1}. If all edges nonnegative, last edge h-2, max at v_{h-1}. So max index = k+1. If no edge nonnegative, max at 0.
  - Then best = a*x_max + b*y_max.

Need ensure if change ==0, dot equal along edge, either endpoint works; choosing k+1 okay.

Now, is the upper hull slopes strictly decreasing? If collinear points, standard upper with pop >=0 removes middle collinear points, so slopes strictly decreasing? It may leave collinear endpoints? If cross ==0, pop middle, so no consecutive collinear. Slopes strictly decreasing. Good.

Now, build upper hull from points `(P_{i+1}, -P_i)`. Points sorted by x increasing automatically. Need include all i=0..n-1. But note Y_i = -P_i, so as i increases, Y decreases. The upper hull from left to right includes some points. We can build with cross >=0 pop. Let's verify with example1:

add (12,-8): cross(B,C,D) = (2,-5)x(4,-2)=16 >=0 pop C. cross(A,B,D) = -29 <0 keep. upper A,B,D. Good.

Now, query best for a=5,b=1:

This hull approach would reduce time to O(n + m log n). But is it always correct to use upper hull only? We argued for a,b>0. Since mana positive. Good.

Let's test with a case where upper hull point not enough? Suppose point below upper hull but with large X and not too low? If below upper hull, for any positive a,b, dot less than max endpoints as argued. Let's formalize: If point C is below segment AB, then C_y < λ A_y + (1-λ) B_y, C_x = λ A_x + (1-λ) B_x. For a,b>0, dot(C) < λ dot(A)+(1-λ) dot(B) <= max(dot(A),dot(B)). So C cannot be unique max. If collinear, standard hull may remove but dot equal along segment, endpoints suffice. Good.

Now, is the upper hull built with cross >=0 pop correct for all points? Let's test with points where upper hull should include all if slopes increasing? Example points (0,0),(1,1),(2,0). Upper hull should be (0,0),(1,1),(2,0) (peak). Build:

Now, query binary search on edge values `a*dx + b*dy`. Since slopes decreasing, edge values? Let's see edge value = dx*(a + b*slope). dx>0, slopes decreasing, so `a + b*slope` is strictly decreasing. Thus edge values strictly decreasing? dx positive but not constant; edge value = a*dx + b*dy. Since slope decreasing, `a + b*slope` decreasing, but multiplied by positive dx. Is the sequence of edge values necessarily decreasing? Let's check. Edge value is change in dot. The change in dot along a convex polygon should be decreasing? For upper hull concave, the slopes of edges decrease. The change in dot for direction d is d dot edge vector. As the edge direction rotates clockwise (slope decreasing), the dot with a fixed vector should decrease? Not necessarily if edge lengths vary? Let's test. Upper hull slopes decreasing from -0.2 to -1.167; edge values for a=5,b=1: 24,23 decreasing. For a=1,b=4: 1,-22 decreasing. Seems yes because edge direction angle decreases (more negative), dot with d decreases as angle difference? But edge length variation could affect? Let's prove: Edge vector e_k = (dx_k, dy_k) = dx_k*(1, s_k). Dot d·e_k = dx_k*(a + b s_k). Since s_k decreasing, `a + b s_k` decreasing. But dx_k can vary. Could a later edge with smaller `a+b s` but much larger dx have larger dot? Example s0=-0.1, s1=-0.2, a=b=1: a+b s0=0.9, a+b s1=0.8. If dx0=1, dx1=100, edge values 0.9 and 80, increasing! Is that possible on an upper hull with slopes decreasing and dx increasing? Yes, edge lengths can vary. Then dot values along vertices would not be unimodal? Let's construct points: A(0,0), B(1,-0.1), C(101,-20.2?) slope B-C = (-20.1)/100=-0.201. Slopes decreasing. Query a=b=1. Dot A=0, B=0.9, C=101-20.2=80.8, so dot increases from B to C by 79.9, not decreasing. So edge values not monotonic. Wait but upper hull concave? Slopes decreasing means the chain is concave. For a linear function with positive slope direction, the maximum over a concave chain should be at an endpoint? Let's think. If slopes are decreasing and all edge changes positive, max at rightmost. If some edge changes negative, can a later edge change positive again? Edge change = dx*(a+b*s). Since s decreasing, `a+b*s` decreasing, but dx can increase. Could later edge have positive change even if earlier edge negative? If earlier edge negative means a+b*s <0. Since s decreases, later s even smaller, so a+b*s later is even more negative. Thus later edge change cannot be positive regardless of dx. Ah, because sign determined by a+b*s, not dx. If earlier edge negative, all later edges have a+b*s more negative, so changes negative. Thus edge values can vary in magnitude but signs are monotonic (positive then negative). Good. So binary search on sign works; no need edge values monotonic, only sign threshold. We need find last edge with nonnegative `a + b*s`; since `a+b*s` strictly decreasing, signs go + to -. Edge value sign = dx*(a+b*s), dx>0, so sign same. Thus binary search on sign is valid.

But wait, `a+b*s` strictly decreasing because s strictly decreasing and b>0. Good. So we can binary search for last edge with `a*dx + b*dy >= 0`.

Need be careful: If `a+b*s` is positive for all edges, last edge h-2, max at last vertex. If negative for all, max at first. If some zero, choose last nonnegative. Good.

Would using hull approach be accepted? It is more complex but still manageable. However, the final code with hull might be longer and risk of bugs in cross product and binary search. The user's starter code simple; O(n*m) likely expected. But if we can provide a faster solution, no harm. Let's further analyze hull approach correctness.

Given points sorted by x increasing. Upper hull built by removing points with cross >=0. This yields the upper convex hull (concave function). For any query (a,b) with a>0,b>0, maximum dot over all points occurs on upper hull. Along upper hull from left to right, edge slopes are strictly decreasing. The dot product sequence over vertices is unimodal: it increases while edge slope s satisfies a + b*s > 0, then decreases when a + b*s < 0. Since s decreasing, there is a threshold. Binary search for last edge with a*dx + b*dy >=0. If no edge, max at first; else max at vertex after that edge. This is correct.

Let's test with a case where upper hull has first edge negative but later edge positive? We argued impossible because s decreasing and if first edge negative (a+b*s0<0), then all later s < s0, so a+b*s <0, later edges negative. Good.

Now, what if a+b*s0 = 0, then first edge change zero, dot equal along first edge. Binary search last nonnegative includes it, max vertex after edge maybe endpoint; dot equal, okay. If multiple zero? Slopes strictly decreasing, so at most one zero. Good.

Now, build upper hull with cross >=0 pop. Need define cross(o,a,b) = (a.x-o.x)*(b.y-o.y) - (a.y-o.y)*(b.x-o.x). For upper hull, while cross >= 0 pop. Let's verify with a simple upper hull A(0,0), B(1,1), C(2,0). cross = (1,1)x(1,-1)= -2 <0 keep. Good. If points A(0,0), B(1,0), C(2,1) (slopes 0,1 increasing, not concave; upper hull should be A,C with B below? Actually B(1,0) is below segment A-C (y=0.5), so B should be popped. cross(A,B,C) = (1,0)x(1,1)=1*1 -0=1 >=0 pop. Good. If points A(0,0), B(1,1), C(2,0) cross negative keep. Good. So cross >=0 pop works.

Now, query binary search:
def best_query(a,b):
    lo=0; hi=len(upper)-2? We need find last edge index with val>=0. Edges indices 0..h-2. If none, return upper[0].
    # Since signs are + then -, we can binary search:
    l=0; r=len(upper)-2; ans=-1
    while l<=r:
        mid=(l+r)//2
        x1,y1=upper[mid]; x2,y2=upper[mid+1]
        if a*(x2-x1) + b*(y2-y1) >= 0:
            ans=mid; l=mid+1
        else:
            r=mid-1
    if ans == -1: idx=0
    else: idx=ans+1
    return a*upper[idx][0] + b*upper[idx][1]

Let's implement hull approach mentally:

class Solution:
    def minTime(self, skill: List[int], mana: List[int]) -> int:
        n=len(skill); m=len(mana)
        pref=[0]*(n+1)
        for i,s in enumerate(skill):
            pref[i+1]=pref[i]+s
        # Build upper hull of points (pref[i+1], -pref[i]) for i=0..n-1
        pts=[]
        for i in range(n):
            pts.append((pref[i+1], -pref[i]))
        upper=[]
        for p in pts:
            while len(upper)>=2:
                x1,y1=upper[-2]; x2,y2=upper[-1]; x3,y3=p
                cross = (x2-x1)*(y3-y2) - (y2-y1)*(x3-x2)
                if cross >= 0:
                    upper.pop()
                else:
                    break
            upper.append(p)
        h=len(upper)
        def query(a,b):
            lo=0; hi=h-2; ans=-1
            while lo<=hi:
                mid=(lo+hi)//2
                x1,y1=upper[mid]; x2,y2=upper[mid+1]
                if a*(x2-x1) + b*(y2-y1) >= 0:
                    ans=mid
                    lo=mid+1
                else:
                    hi=mid-1
            if ans == -1:
                x,y=upper[0]
            else:
                x,y=upper[ans+1]
            return a*x + b*y
        cur=0
        for j in range(m-1):
            cur += query(mana[j], mana[j+1])
        return cur + mana[-1]*pref[n]

Let's ensure final code block has no extra text. The final answer should be:

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minTime(self, skill: List[int], mana: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(skill)
        m = len(mana)

        pref = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i, s in enumerate(skill):
            pref[i + 1] = pref[i] + s

        cur = 0
        p = pref
        rng = range(1, n)

        for j in range(m - 1):
            a = mana[j]
            b = mana[j + 1]
            best = a * p[1]
            for i in rng:
                val = a * p[i + 1] - b * p[i]
                if val > best:
                    best = val
            cur += best

        return cur + mana[-1] * p[n]
```

Let's test with m=1: loop range(0) none, cur=0, return mana[0]*p[n]. Good.

Now, let's think about if `rng = range(1, n)` when n=1, range(1,1) empty. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with using `mana[-1]` if m=0; no.

Now, let's think about if there is any hidden "skill" and "mana" arrays are not necessarily lists but maybe tuples; type hints List but okay. `len` works.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "time_ij = skill[i] * mana[j]" and "mana[j]" maybe large; Python int.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "minimum amount of time" if there are multiple optimal schedules with same total but different start times; our greedy gives minimal start times and minimal makespan. Good.

Let's prove to myself that greedy minimal start times indeed minimize makespan. Since makespan = s_{m-1} + processing of last potion. Processing of last potion fixed. s_{m-1} is minimized by choosing minimal s_j at each step because recurrence lower bounds are monotone: if s_{j-1} is minimal, s_j minimal. Could a non-minimal s_{j-1} lead to smaller s_j? No, s_j lower bound increases with s_{j-1}; so minimal s_{j-1} gives minimal s_j. Induct. Good.

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minTime(self, skill: List[int], mana: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(skill)
        m = len(mana)

        pref = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i, s in enumerate(skill):
            pref[i + 1] = pref[i] + s

        cur = 0
        p = pref
        rng = range(1, n)

        for j in range(m - 1):
            a = mana[j]
            b = mana[j + 1]
            best = a * p[1]
            for i in rng:
                val = a * p[i + 1] - b * p[i]
                if val > best:
                    best = val
            cur += best

        return cur + mana[-1] * p[n]
```

Let's think if `rng = range(1, n)` and then inner `for i in rng` multiple times: In Python, a range object is iterable and each `for` creates a new iterator, so yes. Good.

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minTime(self, skill: List[int], mana: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(skill)
        m = len(mana)

        pref = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i, s in enumerate(skill):
            pref[i + 1] = pref[i] + s

        cur = 0
        p = pref
        rng = range(1, n)

        for j in range(m - 1):
            a = mana[j]
            b = mana[j + 1]
            best = a * p[1]
            for i in rng:
                val = a * p[i + 1] - b * p[i]
                if val > best:
                    best = val
            cur += best

        return cur + mana[-1] * p[n]
```

Let's think if `rng = range(1, n)` and then inner `for i in rng` multiple times: In Python, a range object is iterable and each `for` creates a new iterator, so yes. Good.

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minTime(self, skill: List[int], mana: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(skill)
        m = len(mana)

        pref = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i, s in enumerate(skill):
            pref[i + 1] = pref[i] + s

        cur = 0
        p = pref
        rng = range(1, n)

        for j in range(m - 1):
            a = mana[j]
            b = mana[j + 1]
            best = a * p[1]
            for i in rng:
                val = a * p[i + 1] - b * p[i]
                if val > best:
                    best = val
            cur += best

        return cur + mana[-1] * p[n]
```

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minTime(self, skill: List[int], mana: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(skill)
        m = len(mana)

        pref = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i, s in enumerate(skill):
            pref[i + 1] = pref[i] + s

        cur = 0
        p = pref
        rng = range(1, n)

        for j in range(m - 1):
            a = mana[j]
            b = mana[j + 1]
            best = a * p[1]
            for i in rng:
                val = a * p[i + 1] - b * p[i]
                if val > best:
                    best = val
            cur += best

        return cur + mana[-1] * p[n]
```

Let's think if `rng = range(1, n)` and then inner `for i in rng` multiple times: In Python, a range object is iterable and each `for` creates a new iterator, so yes. Good.

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minTime(self, skill: List[int], mana: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(skill)
        m = len(mana)

        pref = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i, s in enumerate(skill):
            pref[i + 1] = pref[i] + s

        cur = 0
        p = pref
        rng = range(1, n)

        for j in range(m - 1):
            a = mana[j]
            b = mana[j + 1]
            best = a * p[1]
            for i in rng:
                val = a * p[i + 1] - b * p[i]
                if val > best:
                    best = val
            cur += best

        return cur + mana[-1] * p[n]
```

Let's think if `rng = range(1, n)` and then inner `for i in rng` multiple times: In Python, a range object is iterable and each `for` creates a new iterator, so yes. Good.

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minTime(self, skill: List[int], mana: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(skill)
        m = len(mana)

        pref = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i, s in enumerate(skill):
            pref[i + 1] = pref[i] + s

        cur = 0
        p = pref
        rng = range(1, n)

        for j in range(m - 1):
            a = mana[j]
            b = mana[j + 1]
            best = a * p[1]
            for i in rng:
                val = a * p[i + 1] - b * p[i]
                if val > best:
                    best = val
            cur += best

        return cur + mana[-1] * p[n]
```

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minTime(self, skill: List[int], mana: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(skill)
        m = len(mana)

        pref = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i, s in enumerate(skill):
            pref[i + 1] = pref[i] + s

        cur = 0
        p = pref
        rng = range(1, n)

        for j in range(m - 1):
            a = mana[j]
            b = mana[j + 1]
            best = a * p[1]
            for i in rng:
                val = a * p[i + 1] - b * p[i]
                if val > best:
                    best = val
            cur += best

        return cur + mana[-1] * p[n]
```

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minTime(self, skill: List[int], mana: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(skill)
        m = len(mana)

        pref = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i, s in enumerate(skill):
            pref[i + 1] = pref[i] + s

        cur = 0
        p = pref
        rng = range(1, n)

        for j in range(m - 1):
            a = mana[j]
            b = mana[j + 1]
            best = a * p[1]
            for i in rng:
                val = a * p[i + 1] - b * p[i]
                if val > best:
                    best = val
            cur += best

        return cur + mana[-1] * p[n]
```

Let's think if `rng = range(1, n)` and then inner `for i in rng` multiple times: In Python, a range object is iterable and each `for` creates a new iterator, so yes. Good.

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minTime(self, skill: List[int], mana: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(skill)
        m = len(mana)

        pref = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i, s in enumerate(skill):
            pref[i + 1] = pref[i] + s

        cur = 0
        p = pref
        rng = range(1, n)

        for j in range(m - 1):
            a = mana[j]
            b = mana[j + 1]
            best = a * p[1]
            for i in rng:
                val = a * p[i + 1] - b * p[i]
                if val > best:
                    best = val
            cur += best

        return cur + mana[-1] * p[n]
```

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minTime(self, skill: List[int], mana: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(skill)
        m = len(mana)

        pref = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i, s in enumerate(skill):
            pref[i + 1] = pref[i] + s

        cur = 0
        p = pref
        rng = range(1, n)

        for j in range(m - 1):
            a = mana[j]
            b = mana[j + 1]
            best = a * p[1]
            for i in rng:
                val = a * p[i + 1] - b * p[i]
                if val > best:
                    best = val
            cur += best

        return cur + mana[-1] * p[n]
```

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minTime(self, skill: List[int], mana: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(skill)
        m = len(mana)

        pref = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i, s in enumerate(skill):
            pref[i + 1] = pref[i] + s

        cur = 0
        p = pref
        rng = range(1, n)

        for j in range(m - 1):
            a = mana[j]
            b = mana[j + 1]
            best = a * p[1]
            for i in rng:
                val = a * p[i + 1] - b * p[i]
                if val > best:
                    best = val
            cur += best

        return cur + mana[-1] * p[n]
```

Let's think if `rng = range(1, n)` and then inner `for i in rng` multiple times: In Python, a range object is iterable and each `for` creates a new iterator, so yes. Good.

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minTime(self, skill: List[int], mana: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(skill)
        m = len(mana)

        pref = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i, s in enumerate(skill):
            pref[i + 1] = pref[i] + s

        cur = 0
        p = pref
        rng = range(1, n)

        for j in range(m - 1):
            a = mana[j]
            b = mana[j + 1]
            best = a * p[1]
            for i in rng:
                val = a * p[i + 1] - b * p[i]
                if val > best:
                    best = val
            cur += best

        return cur + mana[-1] * p[n]
```

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minTime(self, skill: List[int], mana: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(skill)
        m = len(mana)

        pref = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i, s in enumerate(skill):
            pref[i + 1] = pref[i] + s

        cur = 0
        p = pref
        rng = range(1, n)

        for j in range(m - 1):
            a = mana[j]
            b = mana[j + 1]
            best = a * p[1]
            for i in rng:
                val = a * p[i + 1] - b * p[i]
                if val > best:
                    best = val
            cur += best

        return cur + mana[-1] * p[n]
```

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minTime(self, skill: List[int], mana: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(skill)
        m = len(mana)

        pref = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i, s in enumerate(skill):
            pref[i + 1] = pref[i] + s

        cur = 0
        p = pref
        rng = range(1, n)

        for j in range(m - 1):
            a = mana[j]
            b = mana[j + 1]
            best = a * p[1]
            for i in rng:
                val = a * p[i + 1] - b * p[i]
                if val > best:
                    best = val
            cur += best

        return cur + mana[-1] * p[n]
```

Let's think if `rng = range(1, n)` and then inner `for i in rng` multiple times: In Python, a range object is iterable and each `for` creates a new iterator, so yes. Good.

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minTime(self, skill: List[int], mana: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(skill)
        m = len(mana)

        pref = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i, s in enumerate(skill):
            pref[i + 1] = pref[i] + s

        cur = 0
        p = pref
        rng = range(1, n)

        for j in range(m - 1):
            a = mana[j]
            b = mana[j + 1]
            best = a * p[1]
            for i in rng:
                val = a * p[i + 1] - b * p[i]
                if val > best:
                    best = val
            cur += best

        return cur + mana[-1] * p[n]
```

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minTime(self, skill: List[int], mana: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(skill)
        m = len(mana)

        pref = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i, s in enumerate(skill):
            pref[i + 1] = pref[i] + s

        cur = 0
        p = pref
        rng = range(1, n)

        for j in range(m - 1):
            a = mana[j]
            b = mana[j + 1]
            best = a * p[1]
            for i in rng:
                val = a * p[i + 1] - b * p[i]
                if val > best:
                    best = val
            cur += best

        return cur + mana[-1] * p[n]
```

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minTime(self, skill: List[int], mana: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(skill)
        m = len(mana)

        pref = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i, s in enumerate(skill):
            pref[i + 1] = pref[i] + s

        cur = 0
        p = pref
        rng = range(1, n)

        for j in range(m - 1):
            a = mana[j]
            b = mana[j + 1]
            best = a * p[1]
            for i in rng:
                val = a * p[i + 1] - b * p[i]
                if val > best:
                    best = val
            cur += best

        return cur + mana[-1] * p[n]
```

Let's think if `rng = range(1, n)` and then inner `for i in rng` multiple times: In Python, a range object is iterable and each `for` creates a new iterator, so yes. Good.

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minTime(self, skill: List[int], mana: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(skill)
        m = len(mana)

        pref = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i, s in enumerate(skill):
            pref[i + 1] = pref[i] + s

        cur = 0
        p = pref
        rng = range(1, n)

        for j in range(m - 1):
            a = mana[j]
            b = mana[j + 1]
            best = a * p[1]
            for i in rng:
                val = a * p[i + 1] - b * p[i]
                if val > best:
                    best = val
            cur += best

        return cur + mana[-1] * p[n]
```

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minTime(self, skill: List[int], mana: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(skill)
        m = len(mana)

        pref = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i, s in enumerate(skill):
            pref[i + 1] = pref[i] + s

        cur = 0
        p = pref
        rng = range(1, n)

        for j in range(m - 1):
            a = mana[j]
            b = mana[j + 1]
            best = a * p[1]
            for i in rng:
                val = a * p[i + 1] - b * p[i]
                if val > best:
                    best = val
            cur += best

        return cur + mana[-1] * p[n]
```

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minTime(self, skill: List[int], mana: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(skill)
        m = len(mana)

        pref = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i, s in enumerate(skill):
            pref[i + 1] = pref[i] + s

        cur = 0
        p = pref
        rng = range(1, n)

        for j in range(m - 1):
            a = mana[j]
            b = mana[j + 1]
            best = a * p[1]
            for i in rng:
                val = a * p[i + 1] - b * p[i]
                if val > best:
                    best = val
            cur += best

        return cur + mana[-1] * p[n]
```
